LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

The war has cost U.S. taxpayers an estimated $47.1 billion so far and has left the United States military depleted on a number of levels—from drastically reducing key munitions stockpiles to the destruction of expensive equipment and aircraft. The U.S. is estimated to have used up to 65 percent of its Patriot air-defense interceptors and 80 percent of its Terminal High Altitude Area Defense (THAAD) ballistic missile interceptors since the war began, for example. The U.S. stockpile of Tomahawk cruise missiles is estimated to have fallen from around 3,000 to 2,000 since the fighting started, and the war has reportedly left its stocks of missiles for the Army Tactical Missile Systems, or ATACMS, and precision strike missiles almost exhausted. Around a quarter of the U.S. military’s MQ-9 Reaper drones have also reportedly been lost during the war. Rebuilding these stockpiles will take years. All of this raises questions about U.S. military readiness and capabilities if Trump chooses to return to war post-election. * Washington Is Debating the Wrong AI Risk
  Foreign Policy | 02 Nov 17:46 UTC
  Fixating on low-likelihood, high-impact scenarios misses the forest for the trees.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Washington is gripped by an artificial intelligence (AI) panic, driven by the prospect of superintelligent models delivering our doom. Today’s Nostradamuses include whistleblowers from frontier AI companies—who put the odds of AI killing all humans at greater than one in 10 and warn that insiders expect it could happen before the end of the decade —and anti-nuclear activists, who note that it wouldn’t be the first time humanity built something that could kill us all . The doomsayers may yet be right, but their scenario rests on a long chain of assumptions, each highly unlikely on its own and far less likely to align all at once. This doesn’t mean AI isn’t dangerous. But by fixating on low-likelihood, high-impact scenarios, we miss the forest for the trees: the common, mundane AI dangers already present and actively causing harm. The pressing AI risk Washington should focus on more is automation bias, the phenomenon where humans over-rely on or over-delegate to AI relative to the accuracy of AI models, off-loading cognition to the machine, sometimes even in the face of contradictory evidence. Looking at the elaborate setup, I turned to my program manager and asked under my breath, “How much did we pay for this?” USAID contractors always footed the bill for such events, in my experience. “Nothing,” he replied. “This is their show.” This memory epitomized for me both the promise and dysfunction of my 21 years at USAID. We existed to bring such transformational reform to life, within the governments of our host countries. Yet we were hampered by our own systems and practices. The end of USAID’s operations in September 2025 was a tragedy—for U.S. soft power and influence, for the lives in developing countries that have been lost , and for the shameful treatment of USAID’s foreign and American staff. Lula’s political resilience and longevity are unmatched by most democratically elected politicians. He first ran for president in 1989 and won on his fourth attempt, in 2002. After two presidential terms lasting from 2003 to 2010, a corruption conviction kept him out of the 2018 race that brought Jair Bolsonaro to power. Brazil’s Supreme Court annulled Lula’s conviction in 2021, and he defeated Bolsonaro the following year by less than 2 points. Lula won his fourth term in the contest against Flávio by roughly twice that margin. Since Brazil’s redemocratization in the 1980s, no other politician has been elected president more than twice. * Interactive: Who wants what at the COP31 climate change summit
  Carbon Brief | 02 Nov 16:57 UTC
  Delegates will soon gather in the Turkish resort city of Antalya for UN climate negotiations, against a backdrop of war,… The post Interactive: Who wants what at the COP31 climate change summit appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Delegates will soon gather in the Turkish resort city of Antalya for UN climate negotiations, against a backdrop of war , energy crisis and climate extremes . At COP31, nations will try to find agreement on various issues, including climate finance for developing countries and a global “ just transition ” towards a cleaner economy. This summit is unusual due to its joint presidency, which means that Australia is leading the negotiations while Turkey hosts the conference. Outside the formal negotiations, Turkey is also pushing its own priorities as part of a wider “ action agenda ”, including electrification , oceans and “green industrialisation”. In order to track parties’ positions on key negotiating points and wider issues at COP31, Carbon Brief has analysed around 100 documents and captured them in the interactive table below. The first column shows the countries and UN negotiating blocs , the second shows the topics up for debate and the third indicates specific issues within those topics. The final column indicates the positions that the parties have expressed on each topic. These range from “priority” – meaning the party is likely to be pushing the issue – to “oppose”, meaning it is likely to push against it. This is a “living document” that will be updated over the course of COP31. Please get in touch if you would like to offer additions by emailing [email protected] . Brief explanations of the key issues and jargon-filled language that permeate the talks can be found below the interactive table. At COP30 in Brazil in 2025, nations agreed to establish a practical mechanism to help drive a “ just transition ” to a low-carbon economy. Key aspects of this “Belém-Antalya mechanism” (BAM) – often referred to as the “just transition mechanism” (JTM) – were agreed during the June climate negotiations this year, but many details are missing and dividing lines remain between parties. Parties have disagreed about the scope of the mechanism and whether it should be tied to the provision of finance. Nevertheless, following progress in June within the “just transition work programme” (JTWP), civil society groups highlighted that getting the mechanism up and running is a “key expected outcome” from COP31. Climate finance is always a highly contested issue at UN climate negotiations. Developing countries have successfully pushed for a “work programme” on climate finance to feature on the conference agenda . These parties see this programme as a way to pressure dev
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/interactive-who-wants-what-at-the-cop31-climate-change-summit

* Searching for Jobs, Gen Z Feels Like It’s Staring Into ‘the Void’
  Longreads | 02 Nov 20:25 UTC
  "Hundreds of applications later, even being rejected by an actual human being counts as a victory."
  FULL TEXT: Looking for a job? It’s incredibly tough out there. For young-adult job seekers and college graduates with newly earned degrees across disciplines, speaking to an actual human being during the application process can feel impossible. Reporting for The New York Times , Callie Holtermann describes what members of Gen Z are experiencing and feeling. Isolated, humiliated, and useless, most feel like they’ve thrown their applications into an abyss—and the advice that older generations have given them no longer applies. Some job-seekers have responded with A.I. strategies of their own . Many write the first draft of their résumés themselves, then ask a chatbot to sprinkle in keywords from the job description. Others direct chatbots to compose their cover letters whole cloth. (Ethical or not, it may be a shrewd move: Research suggests that A.I. résumé screeners rate applications written by A.I. more highly than ones written by humans.) Job-seekers described occasionally sitting down for video interviews with A.I. avatars complete with blank stares and too-shiny foreheads. They said they were especially sick of one-way video interviews in which applicants tape themselves answering questions for a hiring manager (or an A.I. system) to assess on their own time. “Could apprentice undertakers solve youth unemployment?” “They are mocked by gen Z for everything from their trainer socks to their mom jeans and selfie technique. Lojo nodded her percussive, patient nod. Christian was talking about how airplanes to the island weren’t safe. There were guns, knives, razors. Lojo continued to listen and nod. Now Christian provided an opening. They really could use a shower. They weren’t interested in shelter, but a shower would be great. So would clothes. Could Lojo help them get pants with a thirty-one- or thirty-two-inch waist? Lojo said that the morning team could bring them clothes. They could go to a facility where they could wash and change and feel fresh. Christian liked this plan. On her tablet, Lojo noted the general area where she had encountered Christian—Tracks 20 and 21—and typed instructions to her team. Both parents, she says, were impacted by the joint traumas of the Nakba and the Naksa. “They never really had the opportunity to process this trauma,” she says. “As a result, our lives — the lives of my brother and I — have been really affected by that trauma. It’s trickled down to us.” Arafat notes that her father’s image of America, where he would ultimately make his home, was shaped as much by what he wanted it to be as by what it really was. “ He projected his ideas of what the West was onto the West, and this is what led him to return here, build a life here. It shaped so much of his future, his family’s future, his grandchildren’s future,” she says. As much as Arafat’s writing has traditionally been influenced by her experience as an outsider, a recent article penned by Arafat in New York Magazine suggested that may have changed. That article, “Habibi City,” documented the growing cultural visibility of SWANA, and particularly Arab communities in New York City. “What’s so inspiring about what is happening in this community is the way that individuals are coming together without the institutional support. It’s just very collaborative and also tied to a political moment,” she says.   New Lines | 02 Nov 11:25 UTC
  The sudden emergence of the label in the U.S. midterms is evidence of a rhetorical exchange between the U.S. and its autocratic allies in the Middle East, as each finds ways to delegitimize, demonize and even prosecute those who might threaten the illiberal status quo of their regimes. The post The ‘Muslim Brotherhood’ Playbook appeared f...
  FULL TEXT: In mid-October, at a lively town hall with Democratic Senate hopeful Abdul El-Sayed in Caro, Michigan, a young man with a square jaw and sandy brown hair stood up to address the candidate. “I have one simple question,” he projected across the crowd. “Will you disavow the Muslim Brotherhood?” El-Sayed quickly replied, “Sure, yeah, absolutely,” before turning the thrust of the inquiry back to the young man, who had identified himself as “Tommy.” “Why are you asking the question?” El-Sayed demanded. “Because they’re a terrorist group,” replied Tommy. El-Sayed went on to give Tommy, who reporting later would show had been a staffer for Republican Sen. Josh Hawley of Missouri, a lecture on John F. Kennedy, faith and the Constitution. But this simple exchange at the beginning of a viral moment revealed how deeply a new epithet has penetrated the political zeitgeist in the U.S. The Muslim Brotherhood has existed for almost a century, but the 2026 midterms have brought it into the mainstream in U.S. politics. It has been trotted out at campaign rallies by President Donald Trump, who has called El-Sayed a “jihadist Muslim Brotherhood member”; invoked by Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who claimed the organization’s rise led to the decline of the Arab world’s contributions to science and literature; and been the subject of a podcast episode by Sen. Ted Cruz of Texas, who claimed El-Sayed was seeking to “infiltrate” Congress to potentially leak classified information to the group, which he described as “radical Marxist Islamists.” Though the label of “Muslim Brotherhood” may be new to a U.S. voter, the playbook that applies it is not. Rather, it is evidence of a rich rhetorical exchange between the U.S. and its autocratic allies in the Middle East, as each finds ways to delegitimize, demonize and even prosecute unwanted elements, particularly those who might threaten the illiberal status quo of their regimes. Throughout its history, the Muslim Brotherhood has been at turns embraced, rejected, used, maligned, persecuted, prosecuted and promoted by states and regimes throughout the Middle East and North Africa for their own ends. Founded by a young school teacher and imam named Hassan al-Banna in Egypt in 1928, the movement began largely as a social organization attempting to counter the influence of Western imperialism by recentering Islam’s role in society through preaching a populist message of traditional values and worker protection against foreign monopol
  https://newlinesmag.com/argument/the-muslim-brotherhood-playbook/

  FULL TEXT: The meaning of ‘now’ might seem rather obvious: it’s the state of awareness you’re experiencing at this very moment, of course. * No iPad Till After Dinner, Mom
  Longreads | 02 Nov 11:00 UTC
  "Seniors are spending more time than ever on their slop-filled feeds. It’s their adult children’s job to save them."
  FULL TEXT: For Intelligencer , Jessica Lucas offers a smart but depressing look at the seniors who are increasingly glued to their screens, mindlessly scrolling YouTube videos and Instagram Reels, often into the wee hours of the morning. It’s hard not to wonder if this constant passive consumption exacerbates cognitive decline. Some of the adult children Lucas speaks with routinely reset their parents’ feeds: fine-tuning algorithms to serve safer content, marking AI slop, or unsubscribing from harmful or fake news channels. Others have gone so far as to set their elderly parents up with teen accounts or secretly curate and monitor their activity. Daniel, a 39-year-old from Virginia, has embarked on a similar project with his own mother, an 81-year-old widow. She has grown so frightened by right-wing videos about crime that she is afraid to walk around her building complex, which has manicured gardens and a koi pond. Every few weeks, he opens her subscriptions page and quietly unsubscribes her from conspiracy and pseudoscience channels, purges her notifications, and spares only the balance-exercise videos for seniors. Then he searches for gardening videos and clips of cats and likes them on her behalf, hoping the algorithm takes the hint. He also signed her up for a Premium account hoping to kill the ads, which had been engineered around her fears, he tells me; one had already talked her into buying life insurance she didn’t need. Even then, the videos he’s trying to shield his mother from come back every few weeks if he doesn’t intervene. “If I could figure out how to get rid of her likes, I would,” he says. “America’s elders need more places to live, but even in liberal enclaves that recognize the problem, developers are facing the fiercest foes of all: America’s elders.” “Devices that monitor seniors for safety are appealing to worried loved ones and underresourced home care agencies.” “A homeless chess hustler, a scholar and an aging recluse found hope in a trashed apartment by Central Park.” “She fell mysteriously ill while living in a remote cabin with my stepdad, and I suspected it was a cry for help. * The self-making world
  Aeon | 02 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Long mocked and misunderstood, Lamarck’s radical idea that organisms shape themselves and their world is making a comeback - by Jessica Riskin Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A pod of humpback whales making its way through Alaska’s Inside Passage. Courtesy of Far North Footage/Getty Images The French naturalist Jean-Baptiste Lamarck was 65 when his great treatise on the transformation of life was published. With Philosophie zoologique (1809), translated a century later as Zoological Philosophy , Lamarck intended to establish an entirely new science, one built on the idea that all living beings are constantly transforming themselves, from the tiniest microbes to the most complex mammals, gaining new forms and new abilities. It wasn’t the bodily structures of organisms that determined their ways of life, Lamarck said, but just the opposite: their ways of life in response to their circumstances gave rise to their changing bodily forms. ‘Can there be any consideration in natural history more important,’ he demanded, ‘or deserving of greater attention, than the one I have just set forth?’ (My translation.) But if you’ve heard anything at all about Lamarck, it’s probably how wrong he was about one animal in particular, the giraffe, and how this giraffe fiasco epitomises his wrongness about everything . Lamarck suggested that, by stretching to reach leaves in high tree branches, giraffes slightly elongated their necks, creating tiny changes that were then handed down to their offspring, accumulating over many generations to produce the long necks we see today. Around the turn of the 20th century, Lamarck’s giraffe idea became a laughing stock when biologists roundly rejected the notion that bodily changes undergone in the course of an individual lifetime could be inherited in the next generation. Illustration from Histoire naturelle, générale et particulière (1749-1804) by Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon. Courtesy the Linda Hall Library It wasn’t only his ideas about long-necked mammals that came under attack. Lamarck’s interest in giraffes was just a passing mention in the midst of his voluminous writings, which set out a revolutionary, field-founding theory: organisms, he claimed, transform themselves and, in doing so, they remake the world. That idea has drawn scorn for more than two centuries. Though Lamarck is often overlooked, his science was foundational to modern biology and informed the theory of evolution that Charles Darwin later developed. Darwin followed Lamarck in believing in the agency of living beings to transform themselves and their environment. But most people don’t know about Lamarck’s influence on Darwin b
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-self-making-world-of-jean-baptiste-lamarck

As fighting intensified across the front lines, an unusual number of mourning posts began appearing in my Facebook feed. Facebook remains Yemen’s public square, a place where people mourn their dead, celebrate milestones, document their lives and argue fiercely over the country’s politics. By early October, as the Houthis advanced deeper into government-held areas of Taiz province and approached the eponymous city itself, my feed had turned into an endless stream of obituaries. Parents grieved for their sons. Wives mourned their husbands. Friends wrote heartbreaking tributes to men they had known since childhood. As I began reading and collecting these posts, Facebook’s algorithm appeared to take notice of my interest. Each time I opened the platform, more photographs of the dead appeared, many shared by people who were not even on my friends list. Scrolling through Facebook began to feel like walking past a wall covered with photographs of the dead. “Hisham, the hero, my brother and beloved martyr, please come back, you troublemaker. My heart aches from your absence,” wrote Shehab al-Shehabi, a journalist, on Facebook on Oct. 13, sharing a photograph of Hisham, who looked barely out of childhood, dressed in a shirt, with a tan tactical vest. Unlike in Houthi-controlled areas, where information about fighters’ deaths appears to be tightly controlled and casualties are often announced through official funerals, families and friends in government-controlled areas openly share photographs, names and personal stories of those killed on the battlefield, making public expressions of grief more visible and spontaneous. * In praise of the dilettante
  Aeon | 30 Oct 10:00 UTC
  A wayfaring cosmopolitan who never gets stuck, the dilettante is the right anti-hero for our hyper-specialised age - by Jay Miller Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jean Cocteau, poet, artist, filmmaker, photographed in 1948 by Philippe Halsman. Courtesy Magnum Photos Why, of all people, defend the dilettante ? An amateur at best, a flighty rascal at worst: why take an interest in someone whose own interests are never more than a passing hobby? These are serious times – why take seriously someone for whom nothing is serious? Merriam-Webster defines the dilettante as ‘a person having a superficial interest in an art or a branch of knowledge’. Encyclopedia.com calls the dilettante someone who cultivates an interest ‘without real commitment or knowledge’. Cambridge settles for someone whose understanding ‘is not very deep or serious’. Collins gives us someone who ‘seems interested in a subject … but does not really know very much about it’. None of it is flattering. Clearly, the dilettante is no aspirational character. No university markets itself as training the next generation of dilettantes; no magazine runs a ‘dilettante of the year’ issue (except, perhaps, for the excellent Idler publication). He – for the dilettante is, historically and in popular imagination, so often a ‘he’ – is in essence an amateur, a role defined by wide interests and narrow expertise. Such is the reputation of the dilettante, assigned and maintained by a culture that treats a single-minded devotion to one pursuit as the only route to a meaningful life. Pick something and stick with it, we are told. It’s not so much an explicit campaign against dilettantism I’m challenging, but rather the diffuse yet relentless pressure to narrow one’s interests, to specialise, to gain expertise, to forgo breadth for the sake of depth. We hear it in the barrage of imperatives to follow your dreams or find your passion uttered at graduation speeches, in self-help guides, and similarly corporate-sounding pep talks. Under this scheme, the specialist is the hero and the dilettante is but a cautionary tale: the person who never committed, never finished, never got good enough at any one thing. But what if the scheme has it backwards? What if the dilettante’s perpetual movement from one thing to another is not a failure of nerve but a different and defensible way of being, one whose exploratory zeal turns out to be just what a hyper-specialised, over-optimised and all-too- serious world needs most? Then the dilettante would turn out to be not a capricious buffoon but a kind of modern anti-hero. That, I argue, is a dilettantism worth defending. F irst, let’s consider
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-the-dilettante-is-the-anti-hero-of-our-over-optimised-age

The scenes sum up the state of independent journalism in Egypt, amid a fraught political climate and mixed signals that swing between a security clampdown and the prospect of a loosening of restrictions. That law is now the new government’s problem, amid growing popular frustration. In the nearly two years since the Bashar al-Assad regime fell, the interim authorities have signed two large real estate deals with Saudi developers on land in the western suburbs of Damascus that the old regime took from farmers in the 1970s and 1980s: Abyat Hills in Qudsaya and Sham View between Qudsaya and Dummar. In late September, the original owners marched on both sites and stopped the machinery. The government responded that the expropriations were legal and all requisite compensation had been paid. Even for people from the area like me, the protests and the public debate since the downfall of the Assad regime have revealed the scale of the issue in ways we had not previously understood. In the towns along the Damascus-Beirut highway alone, residents count tens of thousands of acres taken for state housing under two decrees signed by Hafez al-Assad in the mid-1970s, with thousands more in Dummar. The Assad regime used the expropriation law to build military bases, security branches and loyalist housing around the Syrian capital. Much of the land the regime took is still in the state’s hands, either fenced off or built on. The new authorities have inherited that issue, but, to the farmers of my father’s generation, they have so far shown no intention of correcting a basic injustice. But more than anything, it seemed to violate the laws that for many years had been my way of making sense of the world: the laws of physics. Cause and effect, action and reaction. The post Serbia’s Victorious New Democrats Face an Old Question appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: For the second time in just over a quarter-century, Serbia has seemingly thrown off the yoke of one-man rule. In October 2000, Serbians mobilized — first at the ballot box, then in the streets — to oust the genocidal regime of Slobodan Milošević . In October 2026, it was sufficient for them merely to vote in overwhelming numbers to force the concession of the strongman at the helm. This time Serbians ousted Milošević’s protege, Aleksandar Vučić, and his ruling Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) after their nearly 14-year tenure in government. The vote capped two years of protest that began in November 2024, when a newly renovated canopy at Novi Sad’s railway station collapsed, killing 16 people. An insurgent student movement blamed the deaths on government corruption, which they saw as a hallmark of Vučić’s rule, and their ensuing blockades of universities grew into the largest protest movement in Serbia since 2000. The movement eventually coalesced into the so-called Student List parliamentary coalition. On Sunday, this bloc won 53% of the popular vote and secured around 140 seats in the country’s 250-seat National Assembly, providing it with a comfortable, stable governing majority. Domestically, the Student List ran on a sweeping anti-corruption platform. Its foreign policy agenda, however, is virtually indistinguishable from that of the SNS — at least on paper. Why? Because Serbian nationalism remains, arguably, the most potent political force in the country. The vote that propelled the Serbian List into power is a heterodox amalgam of the country’s moderate, liberal public and the antiregime far right (voters who believe Vučić has been insufficiently hard-line, for instance, in opposing Kosovo’s sovereignty, and would like the country to pursue still deeper ties with Moscow). The same curious combination was evident throughout the two-year protest movement preceding the vote. A very similar coalition toppled Milošević in 2000. This is the bind in which Serbia’s would-be democrats find themselves. Their aspirations to remake the country’s political order depend, in part, on the support of some of the region’s most reactionary elements. Yet the past 40 years suggest that, much as in Russia, Serbia’s regional ambitions and democratization at home are at odds. The reason is institutional. In theory, per this framework agreement, the Lebanese military is tasked with restoring authority over the entirety of the country’s territory — beginning with several “pilot zones” — while Hezbollah disarms. Drive a hundred miles and you’re bound to run into something. Not so in the Midwest. Nothing here interrupts the flow.   FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Jalaa Marey/AFP via Getty Images) The Israeli 18-year-olds who will vote for the first time today were babies when Benjamin Netanyahu was elected prime minister in 2009. With the exception of a short-lived “government of change” in 2021-22, which was led by another right-wing politician, Naftali Bennett, and followed by the brief caretaker premiership of Yair Lapid, they have not known another national leader. Three-quarters of 18- to 22-year-olds identify as right-wing or far-right, according to a poll conducted recently by Channel 12 News, Israel’s most watched mainstream commercial television broadcaster. Political attitudes among Jewish Israelis have been moving steadily to the right over the last 25 years, since the Second Intifada. For the last decade, polls have consistently shown that just over half of Israeli Jews overall identify as right-wing. For years, calling someone a “leftist” has been an insult. One of my secular, liberal Israeli friends, who lives in a normal suburb of Tel Aviv, told me recently that her teenage daughter burst into tears and said she was afraid to bring her school friends home, lest they gain the impression from my friend’s lifestyle that they were leftists. The spike in far-right views among young adults speaks to a confluence of circumstances that include the COVID-19 pandemic, the Oct. 7 Hamas-led attack, the ensuing war in Gaza — and, most significantly, the normalization of the extreme, antidemocratic right. Now, after three years of war, in addition to 17 years of Netanyahu’s methodical suppression and manipulation of the media, far-right ideas have permeated mainstream Israeli society. When Netanyahu brought Itamar Ben-Gvir and Bezalel Smotrich into his coalition government in 2022, the response from more than half the country, including people who identify as right-wing, was horror. Both men are extremist, antidemocratic, violent settlers. Smotrich was arrested and detained for three weeks in 2005 on suspicion of planning to blow up a main arterial road, while the army rejected Ben-Gvir because it considered him too radical for conscription. Secular liberals of greater Tel Aviv might do their army service in the occupied West Bank, but they saw people like Ben-Gvir and Smotrich as alien and a bit repulsive.   The Great Circle Author on Her Omnivorous Habits, E-Reader Renaissance, and Unending Search for “The One.”
  FULL TEXT: Editor’s Note: Passages is a members-only monthly newsletter about life-changing encounters with stories. Each edition of Passages features a short essay in which one of our favorite writers explores their own life as a reader. Our first contributor, Maggie Shipstead, is an accomplished travel writer whose most recent novel, Great Circle , was shortlisted for the Booker Prize and the Women’s Prize for Fiction. Join Longreads today to receive Passages in your inbox , along with our weekly Top 5 story recommendations and other member perks. When I was a kid, my mom called the stack of books on my nightstand a “smorgasbord.” People use a lot of food language when they talk about reading. We devour; we gulp. I like to “graze” as a reader, moving through multiple books simultaneously, dipping in and out according to my mood. Romantic metaphors, too, come in handy, alluding to the vagaries of appetite and compatibility and taste. I would say that I’m still not a monogamous reader. I play the field; I read around. The current count of books I’m partway through is eighteen: eight print books, eight e-books, and two audiobooks. There’s some nonfiction in there: Into the Ice by Mark Synnott, Boom by Michael Shnayerson, and the audiobook of Entitled by Andrew Lownie, which, if you need to be convinced that Prince Andrew is one of the most odious people on the planet, boy do I have the book for you. The majority of my docket is taken up by novels, though. A galley of Emily St. John Mandel’s new book Exit Party is on my nightstand, as is Ian McEwan’s What We Can Know. (Bad title! I hate titles that are unmemorable strings of nonspecific words!) I’m in a race to finish Venetian Vespers by John Banville before Libby, the public library app, takes it back for maybe the fourth time. The Banville is short and enjoyable but has been a victim of the smorgasbord approach, never quite seizing priority. Also on my Libby shelf are, among others, Lovers XXX by Allie Rowbottom, The Original by Nell Stevens, and Villa Coco by Andrew Sean Greer. On my Bookshop.org app, I have The Remembered Soldier , a magnificent novel by the Dutch writer Anjet Daanje that is very long and that I ended up (happily) buying because I didn’t want to race Libby’s loan period. I wasn’t an early adopter of e-books, partly because my first novel was published in 2012, at the height of e-book doomerism, when everyone was saying print books were soon to be a thing of the past and e-books were the enemy. * Love with nowhere to go
  Aeon | 27 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Grief is not only for the dead. When we are unable to honour other profound losses, we become strangers to ourselves - by Derek van Zoonen Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Telemark, Norway, 2011. Photo by Jonas Bendiksen/Magnum Photos I have been grieving my mother for years, even though she is still alive. Sometimes before falling asleep – in that liminal space between waking and sleeping where the self becomes porous – I’d see the image of the sweet and caring woman who would come to say goodnight, her cheeks covered in thick layers of night cream. Then, out of nowhere, the image would flip into the raging woman who was so entangled in her own suffering that she turned her frustration on us. Over the years, that second woman slowly swallowed the first, as she got pulled into my brother’s addiction. It’s hard to say where her own unhappiness ended and my brother’s suffering began, but together they drifted into a parallel reality, cut off from the world beyond their heads. There was no funeral for this. No condolence cards. No flowers. No phone calls. No leave of absence. What was I supposed to mourn? And yet what I felt for them, what I still feel, has the unmistakable gravity of mourning. Can there be grief without death? I have come to think that we grieve much more often than we realise, and that death is grief’s most visible occasion rather than its essence. If that doesn’t match your experience, it may be because we rarely call these other losses by their name. Most of our losses arrive without a funeral. That is the human predicament: we are creatures built to attach, living in a world where nothing stays. Sooner or later, we lose the things we care about. And since we are complex beings with a body, a mind, a community and an environment – beings who love many different things – there is a great deal we can lose, from our physical health to our sense of who we are, from relationships to homelands to the natural world we share with other species. For creatures like us, there is no way around heartbreak: to live is to lose. Grief is what love does when its object is gone. It is love with nowhere to go. Over the past few years I have accumulated many such losses. There is the psychological loss of my father as I knew and loved him: a brutal stroke rendered him incapable of walking and cycling, and cycling was the only thing that could ‘make his thoughts liquid’, as he used to say. There is the loss of old friendships. First you are no longer part of each other’s lives – you just update one another about your life – and then, one day, it becomes awkward to reach out at all. There is the gradual softening of an academic
